(CHEERS)
TALARICO: We were the underdogs in this race. We went up against nearly four years of doubt and disillusionment. We went up against more than $300 million, more than any Senate campaign in American history. We went up against all the negative ads, smear campaigns, foreign bots and A.I. deepfakes that money can buy. Those, those attacks were the final howls of a dying establishment.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: The death rattle of a status quo that knows its days are numbered. They played dirty because they were desperate. But we never lost hope. We never lost faith. And against big money and long odds, we took our power back.
Always, always bet on the underdog. We built a movement unlike any this state has ever seen. We united Texans from all backgrounds and beliefs under one team to take on this broken, corrupt political system. First-time voters, three-time Trump voters, working people from every corner of the state who are fed up and ready for change.
This, this win, this win is for every underdog who's been counted out and left behind. This win is for every teacher and every nurse, for every farmer and every rancher, for every young person who's trying to buy a home, for every parent who's trying to put food on the table. This win, this win is for every Texan who's ever wondered if anyone is listening.
I heard you. We heard you. And tonight the world hears you.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: To the Texans who supported this movement, I am humbled by your trust. And I will work every day to be worthy of it. And to the Texans who didn't vote for me, I will be your senator, too. I will always --
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: I will always serve you. I will always fight for you. This win is not about the Democratic Party or the Republican Party. It's much bigger than that. There is something broken in America. Our economic system is broken. Our political system is broken.
[00:35:01]
Even our relationships with each other feel broken. That's because, that's because the most powerful people in the world want it that way.
My granddad was a Baptist preacher in south Texas, and when I was little, he told me that we follow a barefoot rabbi who gave us two commandments, love God and love neighbor, because, because there is no love of God without love of neighbor.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: Every, every single person bears the image of the sacred. Every single person is holy, not just my neighbor who looks like me, not just my neighbor who prays like me. Not just my neighbor who votes like me. I am called to love all of my neighbors the way I love myself, because, because despite our differences, we all want the same things. A safe neighborhood, a good job, a high quality, well-funded public school, and the ability to see a doctor when we need one.
But the billionaires who own the social media algorithms, who own the cable news networks, who own the politicians fighting on our screens, they want us at each other's throats. They want us focused on how we're different instead of on how we're the same. They want us looking left and right at each other instead of looking up at them. The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom.
(CHEERS) TALARICO: The people at the top work so hard to keep us angry and divided, because our unity is a threat to their wealth and their power. We, we saw it in this race. We saw it in every billionaire funded attack ad. The people at the top divide us by party, by race, by gender, by religion, by culture, so we don't notice that they are picking our pockets, that they are defunding our schools, that they are gutting our healthcare, that they are raising taxes on all of us while they cut taxes for themselves.
It is the oldest strategy in the world. Divide and conquer. But tonight we sent a crystal clear message to the people at the top. We will not be conquered.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: It -- I think it's safe to say that we are tired of being pitted against each other. We are tired of being told to hate each other. It has been 10 years of this kind of politics. Politics as bloodsport, politics as trolling and owning, politics as professional wrestling. It tears families apart. It ends friendships, and it leaves us all feeling terrible all the time.
People across the political spectrum are hungry for a different kind of politics. Not a politics of hate, not a politics of fear, not a politics of division, but a politics of love.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: My, my faith teaches me that love is the strongest force in the universe. Love, love is not passive. Love is not neutral. Love is not weak. Love requires that we stand between the bullies and the bullied. Love requires that we look evil in the eye and not blink. That kind of love has always been a threat to the powers that be. That kind of love is revolutionary. That kind of love is creative.
Hate is predictable. Hate is boring. Hate can't lower our energy bills. Hate can't teach a child to read. Hate can't build a future. But what hate can't do, love can. Love, love, love can turn a student into a scholar.
[00:40:08]
Love can turn a closet into a nursery. Love can turn an enemy into a friend. Love can turn a campaign into a movement. Love, love can heal what's broken in America. We, we can't defeat the politics of division with more division. We can't win that game. We have to change the game. In my experience, when you extend an open hand rather than a closed fist, you'll be surprised by who takes that hand. We, we didn't win this race by alienating our neighbors or shaming our neighbors, or ridiculing our neighbors.
We embodied a new kind of politics, a politics rooted in a deep love for our state, for our country, and most importantly, for all of our neighbors.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: We, we mobilized. We mobilized our 60,000 volunteers in every corner of the state, and together we delivered bottled water to Sand Branch, a community south of Dallas with no running water. We donated school supplies to an elementary school in El Cenizo, a small town on the border. We collected more than 20,000 pounds of groceries for families in Fort Worth who are struggling under the high cost of living.
Before, before the world changes in ways you see on TV or read about in newspapers, the world changes person to person, neighbor to neighbor. In each moment when you choose to be kind, in each neighbor, we choose to love, in each prayer that we say for someone who would not pray for us in return, this, this is how we won Texas. This is a win the size of Texas.
(CHEERS)
TALARICO: And now, and now we --

He also went on to say what we've heard him say now several times since these massive Republican losses that he looks forward to working with the Democrats. Another Democratic call to tell you about this one in Maine, running to replace term limited Democratic Governor Janet Mills is Hannah Pingree, the Speaker of the State House, the daughter of Congresswoman Chellie Pingree of Maine. She defeats Republican attorney Bobby Charles to win the governorship in Maine.
Another hold to tell you about, this one all the way in the Pacific and Hawaii, Josh Green, the incumbent Democratic governor seeking a second term. He gets it, defeating Republican businessman Gary Cordery to win another four years in Hawaii.
Another projection to bring you now, this one in Oregon, incumbent Democratic Governor Tina Kotek was seen as one of the most vulnerable Democratic incumbents seeking re-election as she was hoping for a second term. There's actually a rematch from 2022. Kotek will defeat Republican former state lawmaker Christine Drazan to win four more years in Oregon.
Let's look at a Republican hold now, this one in Idaho, incumbent Republican Governor Brad Little seeking a third term. He gets it. He defeats a Democratic trial attorney Terri Pickens.
One more Republican hold to tell you about this one in South Dakota, incumbent Republican Governor Larry Rhoden seeking his first full term. Remember, he took over for Kristi Noem when she left the governor's mansion to become DHS secretary under President Trump. Rhoden gets it, he defeats former Democratic state Senator Dan Ahlers.
I think we've got another projection to bring you now. I think that's the last state. Yes.

He typically speaks for 90 minutes, two hours, but his overarching theme has been to pretend like I am on the ballot. That is how he chose to try and motivate his base, and now that it is becoming clear that Democrats will have control of the House and the Senate, he says, I wasn't on the ballot and that is why we didn't win.
As these results were coming into play, he did several interviews, and when he was speaking with Fox News, he said essentially that he bears no responsibility for these sweeping losses, that it is what it is. Take a listen to how he framed it.

There was a mood in the air, I guess, and, you know, if you look, I shouldn't feel guilty because virtually I saw you on your show the other night saying that almost never does the sitting President, no matter how good or bad or Republican or Democrat he or she is, but no matter how good the President is, you lose.

(END VIDEO CLIP)
BENBROOK: And the explanation that we've heard from the White House, that we've heard from Trump himself, is about the candidates, the candidate quality. In South Carolina, typically a reliably red state, CNN has projected that the Democrat there, Annie Andrews, will win. Now, Trump had selected the Republican candidate Darline Graham. BERMAN: That's not low double digits.
ENTEN: That is not low double digits. That's a 30-point shift towards the Democratic Party.
I will note Barack Obama won voters under the age of 30 back in 2008 -- obviously a different group of voters under the age of 30 -- by only 34 points. So again, this is better than Obama was doing.
Within that group, what about young men? Young men -- I mean, this is another story of this election. If you look at young men, OK, Donald Trump won this group, I believe, by a point. Look at this shift.
BERMAN: He won.
ENTEN: He won.
BERMAN: He won that group.
ENTEN: He won young men.
Tonight, in the House vote nationally, look at that. We are talking about Democrats winning that vote by 33 points -- a tremendous shift -- an over 30-point shift.
And then, finally, young Latino men. In 2024, Donald Trump easily carried this group.
BERMAN: He won that group.
ENTEN: He won that group. Easily won that group. He won that group. And what we see right here is, look at this. Oh, my goodness gracious. That is a well over 40-point win for the Democrats running for the House.
This is the story. Young voters, young men, young Latino men racing away from Donald Trump and the Republican Party.
BERMAN: Just seismic shifts.
ENTEN: That's exactly right. In the words of Nancy Reagan, they just said no.
BERMAN: They just said no. All right, Harry Enten, stand by.
ENTEN: Thank you.
BERMAN: We're going to talk to you again shortly. Mississippi and Alaska still counting. I need to know much more about that.
ENTEN: We'll go there.
BERMAN: In the meantime, back with Mark Preston and Leah Wright. And just so the control room knows, I think my audio is dead here. So you got to cue me, someone, when this conversation needs to end. But those numbers that we just heard from Harry -- you know, young
Latino men, a group that Donald Trump won two years ago and to all of a sudden have Democrats win by, you know, upwards of 45-plus points. I mean, you don't see shifts like that in polling.
WRIGHT: I mean, no, you don't see shifts like that unless the Republican candidate in this case has really bad policies, really bad programs, has made it part of his agenda to target the very group that he had won over two years earlier. And that group is really upset about that.
[05:50:05]
I think one of the lessons that comes out of 20 -- that comes out of 2024 that Republicans actually don't realize or don't take into account. They formed this new coalition that includes Latinos, but, in particular, Latino men, young men. They bring in a portion of Black men. And it's this idea -- this idea of a big tent, right? He makes inroads with all kinds of groups. Donald Trump makes inroads with all kinds of groups that they never expected to make inroads with.
They take that for granted. They believe that that is the new Republican majority. What happens? That relationship is fragile. It falls apart, in part because these people are transactional. They want something. They expect something from their public servants. And when it comes time to the -- when it comes time for the midterms, it turns out there's not very much to show except for policies, programs, initiatives that have been punitive -- that have hurt these people. So when you ask them, are you better off now than you were two years ago, they can't answer in the affirmative and it shows up in their vote.
MARK PRESTON, CNN SENIOR POLITICAL ANALYST: Well, these kids too are not their parents, Republican voters either, right? I mean, this whole new generation, the 20-somethings of today, you know, are not the 40- somethings of today or the 60-somethings of today where they have this party loyalty, where they grew up in a party system.
I mean, right now they see a very broken country. They see the fact that there is very few job opportunities. They say -- see AI coming over their shoulder right now, taking away jobs and they just wonder what's next. What's for us? Like, how do we even trust Washington? And they see rising gas prices over the past couple of years. They see that -- an economy that is just a mess right now. And they're transactional. They're going to go the way where they think it's in their best interest, just like most people do.
And I will say this, though, about Democrats, too, as Harry was talking about -- like young men and young voters. Democrats did so well with young voters just in general that when they come back to Washington, D.C. -- and we talk about what agenda items they need to talk about it -- they need to talk to young voters specifically.
BERMAN: I will say, I mean, generally speaking, if you have to choose between young voters and older voters, you choose older voters because they show up more. But when you're getting margins like the ones we see tonight of 50 points, it can make a huge, huge difference. Again, I'm going to tell the control room I don't have audio, it's
dead. So someone's going to have to hand cue me when this segment needs to be over here, just so everyone knows.
I do want to ask -- they were talking about it on the big panel moments ago about the president's travel. You know, he did go to Mississippi, yeah. Do we know if that helps to redo a vote that maybe backfired? I mean, I think now you reasonably have to ask if Donald Trump's presence in those states that he visited a lot at the end -- Texas, Kansas, Iowa, the week before Mississippi -- if it did more harm than good. Reasonable question?
WRIGHT: Absolutely a reasonable question, but I think a reasonable answer is to say that it did more harm than good.
And I just point out, because I think, again, with Colom, as we look at the -- you know, at the Mississippi race and how close it is, one of the things that is fascinating is that -- that has come out of this is that Scott Colom's success and completely understated and overlooked until the last week -- until the week before.
One of the things that I think Republicans just completely missed is that the -- is that Scott Colom's judicial appointment -- Scott Colom -- Scott Colom would have been a district judge in Mississippi in 2022 or 2023 if Joe Biden's nomination of him had gone by and hadn't been blocked by Cindy Hyde. What ends up happening is that block allows Colom to actually run for Senate, and it motivates him. It motivates him to -- along with questions around redistricting, questions around gerrymandering, right, and this idea of being -- of African-Americans, in particular, having their votes and their rights stripped in the state of Mississippi.
And so what do they do? They point to the senator -- the incumbent senator. They point to Donald Trump. And so when he shows up to campaign, I don't actually think that generates turnout. I think it actually generates turnout against him in order to support Colom, who has been at this for at least four years.
BERMAN: One of the questions that I have outstanding right now is we saw the redistricting efforts, the mid-cycle gerrymandering just backfire -- just blow up in Republicans' face, netting maybe two seats. There were two seats in the entire country that were redrawn that Republicans may have won and Democrats won tons, tons, tons of the ones that were redrawn as a result.
Do you think, Mark, that Democrats, when they're in power holding the trifecta in some of these states where there might be seats to be gained --
PRESTON: Right.
BERMAN: -- will push this redistricting? Now that Republicans opened the door will Democrats push it further the next two years?
PRESTON: I think they want to push it forward. I don't know if it's a smart political thing to do. I mean, again, I think that they need to come in. They're going to have upwards of 20 people running for president -- running for their presidential nomination. They need to come to Washington and try to get some things done.
[05:55:08]
I think if it looks like that, they're trying to still engage in the same kind of theatrics that Republicans did, I don't think that's necessarily going to help them. It doesn't mean they're not going to do it, though, for sure.
BERMAN: You think there will be a call? You know, you seem tuned into these young voters, sort of the grassroots. Do you think there will be a call among some grassroots Republicans to go ahead -- sorry, Democrats to do this?
WRIGHT: I think there will be a push to find ways to protect the voting rights and the voting responsibilities and voting privileges of mass groups of people who have had those rights, who have had those privileges and -- those taken away or who have seen disenfranchisement actually become a thing.
BERMAN: All right.
WRIGHT: Um --
BERMAN: Leah Wright, Mark Preston, stand by for one minute. Our special coverage of election night, now election morning in America, continues right after this quick break, including updates on Mississippi and Alaska. They're still counting. Stay with us.
(COMMERCIAL)

## [09] Dems Flip 7 Seats to Win Senate; Alaska, Mississippi too Close to Call; CNN Projects El-Sayed will Win Michigan Senate Race

JOHN BERMAN, CNN ANCHOR: All right. Welcome to CNN's special live coverage of election night in America. It has officially become midterms 2026, midterm morning 2026. This historic night turned into this historic morning.
[06:00:29]
Just a gargantuan shift in power. Democrats will take control of both the U.S. House and the U.S. Senate with sizable margins and with races that we still can't make projections in, because they're just too close. Mississippi and Alaska.
I want you to take a close look at Mississippi. If you've been sleeping, ho, boy. Mississippi, just 43 votes separate the Democrat, Scott Colom with the Republican incumbent, Cindy Hyde-Smith. This was seen as a Republican safe seat. Not safe at all right now. With 925,000 votes or so counted, just 43 votes separate them. Much more to come on that in just a second.
And in Alaska, with 72 percent in, the incumbent Republican, Dan S. Sullivan, leads by a little bit less than a point over the Democrat, Mary Peltola. She has 99,000 votes. He's got 100,000 votes.
You will note there are two other candidates on that list. Very important, because if no one gets more than 50 percent, it goes to ranked-choice voting, and the votes for the third and fourth place candidates, their second choices, they will matter quite a bit.
While we wait on Mississippi and Alaska, CNN projects that Democrats already will control 54 seats in the U.S. Senate. That was, like, the stretch goal for Democrats going into the night, and they could get even higher than that. They will have room to spare in the U.S. Senate.
And that's because they dominated in traditionally red states: South Carolina, Texas, Iowa, and Kansas. Look at those winners there.
In South Carolina, the Democrat, Andrea [SIC] -- Annie Andrews, defeated senator Darline Graham and did it pretty handily, actually. Again, another race that not many people saw coming, although "The New York Times" had them tied in polls just a week ago.
In Texas, James Talarico will be the first Democrat to represent the state in 38 years. His lead right now, like 12 points, a huge victory for Democrats in Texas.
In Iowa, Josh Turek flipped Iowa's open Senate seat there. He defeated Congresswoman Ashley Hinson for that open seat. Again, a Democrat hasn't held that since Tom Harkin in Iowa. A big win.
And then Kansas. What about Kansas? Adam Hamilton, the first Democrat elected to the Senate from Kansas since 1932, which --this just in -- was a long time ago.
In the House, CNN projects that Democrats have locked up 231 seats. Democrats have locked up 231 seats. But that doesn't even begin to tell the whole story there, because that's just the baseline here. They could win upwards of 30 more. Thirty more seats. There could be huge margins for the Democrats in the House, which we'll get to in just a moment.
First, let's go over to Harry Enten and the Magic Wall, and let's talk about the races we still haven't called yet, starting with Mississippi.
HARRY ENTEN, CNN CHIEF DATA ANALYST: Let's go to Mississippi. Has not elected a Democrat to the United States Senate since 1982, John Stennis. Look at this: 43 votes. Forty-three votes separate the Democrat and Republican here in the great state of Mississippi.
And I will note one thing that I think is so notable. Johnny B. and I were talking about this. Still plenty of votes to be counted in Hinds County, Jackson, where they -- I believe they have stopped the vote count here.
BERMAN: "The Mississippi Free Press" reported early this morning that they had halted the vote counting, bone to sleep for a little bit. Presumably, they'll start counting again soon. We could get more numbers in from Hinds County.
ENTEN: Right. And when we look and see in the state where it's 90 percent, or reporting 90 percent or less of the vote, you can see it's a pretty even mixture of red and blue.
And the other question that we have that is outstanding is how many provisional votes are left in Mississippi? We think there could be many of them. And those have traditionally been more blue than the initial election day vote/mail vote combined in that state of Mississippi.
BERMAN: And maybe thousands, by the way.
ENTEN: Could be thousands, could be tens of thousands. We don't know at this point.
BERMAN: All right, Alaska.
ENTEN: Let's go to Alaska. We're going to go from the Southeast to very much, not the Southeast. We'll go up to Alaska.
All right. What do we have here? We've got Dan S. Sullivan, the incumbent, with a little bit less than a 1,700-vote lead over Mary Peltola, the Democrat, at 48 percent of the vote, or a little bit less than 48 percent of the vote.
The key to this whole entire situation in Alaska is the winner needs 50 percent of the vote plus one vote. Ergo, right now, a plurality doesn't do it for you.
They have ranked-choice voting in Alaska, whereby the lower candidates on your screen right here, if the -- if the voters who chose them No. 1, they get their votes reallocated to their second -- second choice, third choice, et cetera.
[06:05:08]
Obviously, the other Dan Sullivan, Daniel J. Sullivan, be interesting to see if you can add those votes to Dan S. Sullivan's total.
Here's the thing I want you to keep in mind about Alaska, though. This is going to go on for a while. And oftentimes in Alaska, the votes that come in later come from the outlying areas, the rural areas.
Here's the thing, though: unlike the lower 48, the outlying areas in Alaska tend to lean to the left, tend to lean blue. We'll see if that's the case this time around. But there is no reason to think that Mary Peltola is out of this game whatsoever.
BERMAN: Just before we go from the wall here, just show me the House races right now. How many races the Democrats currently lead in.
ENTEN: Bluenami, bluenami. Look at this: 263. Two hundred and sixty- three. They entered the evening -- what, Johnny B.? -- At about 215 back in the 2024 election.
That is a gain. That is a gain, potentially, of nearly 50 seats in the U.S. House of Representatives. It would represent one of the largest gains, or, I believe, the largest gain since basically 1974. That, of course, was the Watergate midterms. Richard Nixon being forced to resign that August.
BERMAN: Again, these are where they're leading right now, not called, but it gives you a sense of how much more growth there is in the House.
You're going to put something else up?
ENTEN: I was just going to note that the flips, the flips from 2024. Look at this. It's blue across the map. This isn't just one region. This is not just deep. It is incredibly wide, as well.
BERMAN: And these are the only two districts you're looking at here. These are two districts that the Republicans gerrymandered, redistricting.
ENTEN: Correct.
BERMAN: That's all they got out of it. ENTEN: That's all they got. And those races are extremely, extremely
close at this time.
BERMAN: All right. Harry Enten, stand by. Put Mississippi Senate back up here.
ENTEN: Sure.
BERMAN: Because as we put that up, I'm going to go over to Katelyn Polantz here at the voting desk to get a sense of where these votes are. What happens now in Mississippi? What happens in a state where just 43 votes separate the two candidates?
KATELYN POLANTZ, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Well, John, even if those 43 votes gets smaller -- say the margin goes down to 1 or 2 -- it's tough news for whichever person, Colom or Hyde-Smith, is losing at that point, because there's no automatic recount in the state of Mississippi.
There also isn't the ability for either candidate to request a recount. It would have to go through the court system, and a judge would have to order a recount for there to be one in Mississippi.
Now, even with that happening, it's going to take a little bit of time for us to see what the votes may be, because until November 10th, there will still be two types of ballots that would be coming in to the election counting facilities, to the election counting administrators in Mississippi.
Those two types of ballots. There's the absentee ballots. So, those are people that would especially request a ballot, like a senior citizen, a soldier serving abroad. They would be able to mail those in by election day, so yesterday.
They can be received in Mississippi up for five days after election day, because -- and you asked about this earlier -- the Supreme Court decided in June that Mississippi, specifically, their state law, could stand, allowing the receipt of ballots up to five days after election day.
But I'm told by our decision desk that that is ultimately going to be a pretty small number. And the number we should actually be watching is the number of provisional ballots in Mississippi.
So, people who don't have a voter I.D. or, say, who aren't on a voter roll. They will have up until November 10 to cure their ballots. So, show their I.D. to an election official. They've cast provisional ballots. Those could be counted to that time.
And then the crucial date, November 13. That's when the counties send their results to the state, and the state decision, or the state certification that happens December 3.
BERMAN: So, Harry Enten just pointed out: still some counties with some votes to count, the regular votes to count. We're watching that very closely.
But these provisional ballots could be thousands. They could make a huge difference, as well.
Katelyn Polantz, thank you very much for that update.
All right. One of the races, obviously, we've been watching the last few days very, very closely, was in Michigan, where CNN projected some time ago that the Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, will win that race. He will be the next senator from the state of Michigan.
You can see right there, you know, Abdul El-Sayed a few weeks ago predicted that he was going to win that race by seven points. I want you to look at the margin right now, just under seven points.
Let's go to CNN's Brian Todd, who is in Michigan in Detroit. Talk to us about what you've seen there over the last 48 hours or so.
BRIAN TODD, CNN CORRESPONDENT: Well, John, this has been one of the most bitter and bruising campaigns of the midterm cycle, one of the most bitter campaigns that this state has ever seen. And we're just coming out the other end of it this morning.
Now, as of now, just after 6 a.m. on Wednesday morning, Mike Rogers has not formally conceded this race. But as you just projected, though, you just talked about those numbers: 52 to 45 percent; 95 percent of the vote counted in Michigan.
[06:10:04]
We projected this race for Abdul El-Sayed several hours ago. Mike Rogers, not formally conceding.
It was interesting. Roughly 1 a.m. in the morning, he came out and he told his -- his supporters to go home, but said he still saw maybe a path to victory. But it just wasn't to be for Mike Rogers.
We'll see if he says something to Abdul El-Sayed, maybe later today. If he calls him, we'll see if he concedes and what -- what goes on there.
But again, this has just been a bitter campaign.
Abdul El-Sayed appeared with his wife, Dr. Sarah Jukaku, on stage a little after 1 in the morning and talked to his supporters. He said the bad guys lost and, quote, "We beat a machine." Here are some of his other remarks.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)
ABDUL EL-SAYED (D), MICHIGAN SENATOR-ELECT: Now, as I was campaigning, some of you would come up and ask me, How come you don't hit back? How come you don't call them racist or hateful?
Because here's the thing. I didn't run for myself. And in truth, they weren't targeting me. They were just -- weren't just attacking me. They were targeting and attacking you. They were targeting and attacking the very idea of America. So, they turned this race into a simple choice. They want an America
that is too small for some of us. We know that America is big enough for all of us.
(END VIDEO CLIP)
TODD: And, of course, he's referring to the attacks on his faith by President Trump and Trump's allies all throughout this campaign. They're associating him with the Muslim Brotherhood, which, of course, Abdul El-Sayed has said that's completely not true. And he's denounced the Muslim Brotherhood. He hopes to move on from that when he goes to Washington now.
What was also, of course, a very bitter point in this campaign was the controversy with his primary opponent, Haley Stevens, who he narrowly beat to win that primary in August. She, of course, came out on Sunday and endorsed Mike Rogers. That was very controversial at the time.
But, John, I've got to tell you, we talked to dozens and dozens of voters in Detroit, Ypsilanti, Royal Oak, Michigan, and in Pontiac, Michigan, over the past three days. All the voters we talked to said Haley Stevens's endorsement of Mike Rogers did not sway their vote at all.
I can also say -- this is interesting. We just got word from the Detroit City Clerk's Office. At the spot where we were yesterday, the Deport [SIC] -- Department of Elections in Detroit, there was a line out the door of new voters coming in, registering for the first time.
We talked to a lot of them. That line snaked down the street. The Detroit City Clerk's Office just told us that it was 1,000 new voters who came to the Detroit Department of Elections yesterday to register and vote for the first time.
That's how passionate people became in this race -- John.
BERMAN: New voters, very much the model, very much the design of the Abdul El-Sayed candidacy right there.
Brian Todd for us in Detroit this morning. Great to see you.
Joining us now is Michigan Congresswoman Debbie Dingell, the Democrat. Get any sleep?
REP. DEBBIE DINGELL (D-MI): About as much -- as much as you got. So, no. But it's a new day and a lot of good things ahead.
BERMAN: OK. Explain to me what you learned overnight with this victory of Abdul Sayed -- Abdul El-Sayed. What does it tell you this morning?
DINGELL: Well, so, first of all, I told you all on Saturday that I thought that he would win and that it would be a 2- or 3-point race.
By Sunday afternoon, after Haley Stevens made that very surprise announcement, and I was out at many places, I began to feel a shift of people who had been uncertain, undecided, were maybe going to stay home, that they were going to go and vote.
And by Monday, I was very comfortable, and I knew that Abdul would win.
I think there was a -- it backfired on what she was trying to do. And you had two communities that were -- didn't know Abdul as well as they should.
You've talked about the Jewish community regularly. The African American community, for a variety of reasons, some historical reasons of -- of tension, maybe, between the Arab American and African American community. People don't like people going to churches. And on a day that's a traditional for African American community, a Democratic day.
There just was a lot of -- people were angry on -- on Monday.
So, I think his -- you see the seven-point spread that he predicted. And I think that's what you saw.
I also think that we've got to not overread in this race, but it also offers promise for maybe some healing, of some traditional tensions.
[06:15:06]
And we all have to commit now. I'm going to work very hard with Abdul to stand up to hate wherever and whenever we see it coming out of a horrific, bruising primary, or general election.
BERMAN: I got you. It's early still. I want to take two things you said there and dig in a little deeper.
You said don't overread into it. What's the risk? Overread into what? What's the risk?
DINGELL: So, he did win. We saw, but we saw wins across the country. And he -- he will be a leader. There is no question.
But Democrats have to deliver. So, I -- two years ago in the Donald Trump win, people were not happy with the direction that the country was going. They wanted to see a Congress work together. And they wanted us -- they wanted to see us deliver for working men and women.
So, what I heard last night in the Senate races and in the House races, is we've got to work together. They are hurting from the cost of food, and gasoline, and rent, and mortgage payments. And they don't know if they can afford to go to the doctor or the medicine. And they're disgusted with the corruption in Washington.
And I think we've got to be careful on that and what that means. I think we've got to hold people accountable, but not make it into drama shows.
Because we have a presidential election in two years that we need to win, not want to win, need to win, to keep this country going on a better course. And people are expecting us to deliver, not fight in the way they watched the Republicans do the last two years.
BERMAN: OK. And you talked about healing. What do we want to hear from now Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, in terms of healing? How much should he do on that front?
DINGELL: Well, I think it's got to be all of us. I think that the ugliness on the television ads and the attacks on people of faith and religion calling, implying at times that people of -- of one faith were all terrorists were inexcusable.
But the Jewish community is clearly unsettled. You've heard that as you've been covering that for the last two months. We have to make sure that we know they know that we're going to be there, that we're going to fight to make sure that they are safe, that -- recognize the antisemitism is on the rise and that we're going to fight to protect them.
And that no one is going to be attacked because of their faith. And that everybody's going to be treated with respect and dignity, and that they can count on Democrats to make sure they are safe.
BERMAN: If you'll allow me to ask this, what do you think is next for Haley Stevens?
DINGELL: I don't know. Look, this has been one of the saddest weeks of my life.
I'm -- I'm happy, obviously, by the outcome of last night. I haven't had a voice for two weeks, as you know, because I've been working. Well, I have a voice, and I still make it heard. But it's been pretty scratchy.
Haley is going to have to sort it out. She is someone I've cared for since I first started working with her in the 2000s. But what she -- she's going to have to sort out what her role is and what she's going to do next.
BERMAN: Congresswoman Debbie Dingell, Democrat from Michigan. It has been an extraordinary few weeks for you. You've been in the middle of a lot of things I don't think anyone ever could have predicted.
We appreciate you getting up early and talking to us, even if you got any sleep last night. Thank you very much.
So, we're going to continue to watch the votes come in from Alaska and Mississippi, two races that we have not called. And in Mississippi, just 43 votes separate the two candidates.
Our special live coverage of -- of "Midterm Morning 2026" continues after a quick break.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)

South Carolina, the first time a Democrat has won a Senate race since 1998. North Carolina is the first Democrat elected to the Senate from there since 2008.

OK. So, all of that you list out, and I left out some. How striking is the repudiation of President Trump's first two years in office that has just been laid out before us?

ASHLEY ALLISON, CNN POLITICAL COMMENTATOR: There's no space for debate at this point, but I'll just say two years ago, when Donald Trump won election, many of us who identify as Democrats were told that it was a mandate.

BOLDUAN: David, what the president said last night was quite a lot. He stopped posting so far for the last few hours. We'll see --

BOLDUAN: -- when he does, when he does wake up and what he does have to say. But I want to play for everyone what the president's reaction was to these massive Republican losses. Watch this.
(BEGIN VIDEO CLIP)

They have not gotten this high up in about 35 years in terms of the number of seats, if in fact that holds.

ENTEN: We can absolutely look at the House seats that are flipped, and you can see it right here. It is across the political map. From the Northeast in New York, Pennsylvania, New Jersey, all the way out to California, plenty of seats in the South as well.
And the only two districts that you see in red on your screen, those were in states that were redistricted. Texas Republicans redistricted. Tennessee Republicans redistricted. And even down there in Texas, I mean, just look at the margins here, they're so, so small.

BERMAN: That list you were looking at right there, these states are states where someone's, where people are leading right now, not necessarily what's been called. But, again, in terms of gerrymandering, this might be all Republicans get.
ENTEN: Exactly right. And in none of the states in which they're redistricted do they actually have a net gain pickup overall in those states.
BERMAN: Very quickly, governors. ENTEN: I just want to point out governors here very quickly, and the reason I want to point out governors is this. It's very simple. I will note that that would be the largest such number for Democrats since the early mid-1990s.

In the meantime, one of the biggest stunners of the night unquestionably South Carolina, the Democrat, Annie Andrews, defeated Trump-backed Senator Darline Graham. Not just Trump-backed, but really Trump-picked Senator Darline Graham, the sister of the late Senator Lindsey Graham.
Let's go to CNN's Dianne Gallagher in Charleston. Dianne, Annie Andrews last night had a lot to say in her, frankly, victory speech.
DIANNE GALLAGHER, CNN NATIONAL CORRESPONDENT: Yes. And, look, John, Annie Andrews made history last night, not just as the first woman to ever be elected to the U.S. Senate from the state of South Carolina, but also the first Democrat since 1998, so the first one this millennium.
Now, look, Annie Andrews, a doctor and a mom who cares about affordability. That is how she pitched herself to South Carolina voters over the past 18 months and managed to flip this seat blue, the seat that was held by the late longtime Senator Lindsey Graham, by beating his sister, Senator Darline Graham.
Now, look, Andrews ran initially acknowledging it is an uphill battle for a Democrat, but she continued this disciplined campaign that we've seen by -- from a lot of the Democrats in the southern states, focused on gas, groceries, and healthcare while highlighting a frustration with Washington and corruption.
And it was that there that she even alluded to in her victory speech, but she focused on in these closing days by aggressively courting moderate and disaffected Republicans, frustrated not just with the general environment, but also how President Trump involved himself in the special primary here that ended up with Darline Graham as the candidate.
She noted that in her victory speech while talking about what this night means.

ANDREWS: You have shown the nation that in this era of partisan divides, of vitriol and name-calling, in a politics dominated by billionaires and special interest groups and insiders in Washington, D.C., that we will not bow down.

GALLAGHER: Now, again, Andrews initially was running against Lindsey Graham before he suddenly passed away in July. It might just be beginning.
BERMAN: Yes. Senate terms are six years. All of these races in these deep red states, six years is a long time. These are big, impactful victories for Democrats.

Let's go over to Kate Bolduan and an all-star cast. Kate?

You ran against the late Senator Lindsey Graham in 2020, and you came up ten points short. What is the big difference looking at 2026 and this big win now for Annie Andrews?

But, you know, in politics it's all about timing. And I said, if we have a big blue wave like we had in 2006, 20 years ago we can win this U.S. Senate seat. And we knew Donald Trump wasn't on the ballot, and when you have Democrats running in every state house, every statewide, every Congressional race, it can also raise the boats of all those running on a statewide basis.
And then we had one heck of a candidate in Dr. Annie Andrews. And to be quite honest, the candidate quality on the Republican side was lacking. Darline Graham barely fundraised. She barely did any events. And all of those stars aligned, and we were able to take advantage. Annie Andrews was successful.
Jermaine Johnson, who ran for governor, came so close. It was the closest governor's race that we've had in 20-plus years, but we flipped a state -- we flipped a Congressional race, almost two. Joe Wilson almost went down in terms of his Congressional race, and we broke the Republican supermajority here in South Carolina. And that's what you get when you compete everywhere and you give people a choice.

And so you can walk and chew gum at the same time, but we got to focus on what's important to the American people, and that's their pocketbooks.

Ossoff Re-Elected in Georgia, Beat Mike Collins by 18 Points; Keisha Lance Bottoms Becomes First Black Female Governor in America; Sen. Collins Loses Reelection in Maine to Dem. Troy Jackson

Annie Andrews is the first Democrat to win a Senate seat there in 28 years. And in Kansas, the pastor-turned-politician Adam Hamilton wins. That state hasn't elected a Democratic senator since 1932.

KEN MARTIN, CHAIR, DEMOCRATIC NATIONAL COMMITTEE: Well, it was a blue tsunami, to say the least. I mean, it was just a great victory for Democrats across the board. And we're winning in states throughout the South and throughout the country.

WOLF BLITZER, CNN ANCHOR: I'm Wolf Blitzer, and this is CNN's special live coverage of Election Day in America continued.

Welcome to our special live coverage, Election Day in America continued.
[10:00:03]

In Michigan, Abdul El-Sayed will become the first Muslim senator in U.S. history despite the last-minute endorsement drama in that race as we all watched.
In South Carolina, Annie Andrews will be that state's first woman elected to the U.S. Senate. And in Kansas, Pastor Adam Hamilton beat Senator Roger Marshall. That state hasn't elected a Democrat to the U.S. Senate in nearly a century.
In Maine, a logger is headed to the Senate. Troy Jackson beat five- term Republican senator Susan Collins. And in Ohio, Sherrod Brown is returning to Washington. It's a major comeback. Sullivan, and that lead is 600 votes. That's a flip. Sullivan had been on top about an hour ago. Now, Peltola on top. It's only 600 votes, though, and 74 percent of the vote has been counted, so a lot more to come in.
Keep in mind, we had talked about many reasonable scenarios where Alaska was the deciding state to determine who actually controlled the Senate, and that if Peltola won, we might be waiting several weeks. As shocking there we're even discussing this, but it is a dead-even tie right now between the Democratic challenger, Scott Colom, and the incumbent Republican, Cindy Hyde-Smith. Right now, 43 votes separate the two and 96 percent of the vote counted. And if they win off upsets in these two races, as I said, that would be the 55th and 56th Senate seat, something that nobody had predicted and obviously a stunning rebuke of President Trump.
And, Wolf, we're awaiting a possible press conference there in Mississippi from the Democratic candidate. We'll see if that happens here in these next few minutes.
BLITZER: Anxious to see if that happens indeed. All right, Erin, thank you very much.
Let's break all this down with CNN's Phil Mattingly. He's here with me over at the magic wall. A resounding and historic victory for the Democrats. Take us a little inside some of these questions that are still outstanding.
PHIL MATTINGLY, CNN CHIEF DOMESTIC CORRESPONDENT: Let's start with where Erin left off, and that's Mississippi. We've been waiting all morning for any new vote to come in this race that, as Erin was just laying out, is I don't know that you can get closer than 43 votes between the incumbent, Cindy Hyde-Smith, and her Democratic challenger --
BLITZER: And that's with 96 percent of the vote in.
MATTINGLY: 96 percent reporting. What I've been waiting for and watching and wondering about throughout the course of the morning is right is right over here in Hinds County. This is the home of Jackson.
[10:05:00]
This is a county that Democrats, even in traditional Republican-heavy years, still roll up a lot of big vote.
And right now, the Democrat here is running up a margin that is almost double what they normally do in this county. There's 90 percent reporting here. Normally, that looks like a lot, but with 10 percent outstanding in a race that's separated by this much and is this far into being reported, this will be a significant, significant vote batch whenever it comes in.
They stopped. It was reported they stopped counting at one point earlier in the night. We are waiting to see on that. We'll wait to see if there's a press conference going forward. Where the vote's outstanding, still very much a toss-up race when you actually do the math.
Then I want to go back into Alaska real quick. We saw the only vote we have gotten in these two races came in this race. It put Mary Peltola ahead by 626 votes, 74 percent reporting. There's a lot of vote outstanding in all of the different kind of House precincts in which Alaska uses to report, so we're going to wait and see how that plays out going forward.
As Erin noted, the key number for whoever wins is going to be can you get above 50 and avoid the ranked choice process that would take 15 days to play out, so continuing to watch that as well.
Big picture, Wolf, look, this is stunning, leading in nine seats in nine Republican seats. Seven have been called as flips. You want to get even more into the weeds here. Of those seven that have been called as flips states that Trump won by ten points or more, five of them, five of the seven flips are states that Trump won by ten points or more. In the House, there's 30-plus districts right now that Trump won by ten points or more that Democrats are leading in or have been called. It's a Democratic blowout any way you slice it.
BLITZER: It certainly is. All right, Phil Mattingly, thank you very much. I want to go over to the voting desk. Omar Jimenez, you're taking a closer look at Mississippi, too. What are you seeing?
OMAR JIMENEZ, CNN ANCHOR AND CORRESPONDENT: Yes, because, I mean, the margin is down to literally a few dozen people right now.
So, we want to just go through the two categories of votes still left to count and what could actually make the difference here. So, for starters, we are now waiting on mail-in and absentee ballots to come in, and, really, as long as they're postmarked by Election Day, they can arrive up to 10 -- up until November 10th, five business days.
But what could be more significant is the provisional ballots here. Provisional ballots are typically cast when there's a question about voter's eligibility, so those ballots, known as affidavit ballots in Mississippi, are held for counting until officials are sure that the vote can be accepted.
So, what are those reasons? Could be a voter forgets their I.D. at home, doesn't present acceptable ID, could be a voter isn't immediately showing up in the voter rolls, or it could be records show that they voted previously on Election Day or absentee. They can cast one of these provisional or affidavit ballots that would then be investigated further by election officials.
So, how many are we talking about here? Remember that margin in the vote here, just a few dozen. The Scott Colom campaign estimating up to 15,000 affidavit votes outstanding in his race with Cindy Hyde-Smith. Obviously, when you look at the margin of a literal football team, that movement in any direction could make a huge difference here.
And, look, this is going to be a bit of a crunch because the deadline for all this as well is November 10th for the absentees and these provisionals. It's how long people have to go to their circuit clerk's offices to present proper I.D., for example, for any of those ballots. But then, because we are talking about a tight margin in the vote count, many states, that would trigger an automatic recount if this is where it ends up. Not here. They do not have any automatic or requested recounts. That can only happen through a court order if there is a legal challenge.
And then on those grounds, it would be a tight window when you consider that previous November 10th deadline for the absentees and affidavit ballots. The county certification deadline is November 13th. The state certification deadline is December 3rd.
So, Democrats obviously looking for another pickup. This one would be incredibly significant, but it looks like it may come down to these votes that are still outstanding in this razor-thin margin.
BLITZER: And they got to count all those votes too. That's going to take some time.
All right, thanks very much, Omar. I appreciate it very much. Erin, over to you. BURNETT: All right. Well, Wolf, we do have some breaking news just in, and I'll tell you what it is. I mean, these are incredible things. That gives everybody chills, right, as an American.
And yet even in New York State, that was a contested governor's race, people thought it might be single digits. Democrat wins by 20 points, double maybe what some had expected, if not more in terms of her margin of victory. Louisiana, they hold on to by two points. What is happening?
WENDY DAVIS, PRESIDENT AND CEO, BLUEPRINT TEXAS PAC: Yes. This was a change election obviously. It was absolutely a repudiation of Trump. BURNETT: Well, like you were doing here in Texas, right? It didn't just happen.
DAVIS: What we've been doing here in Texas, you know, from the bottom of the ballot all the way up you can't build the wins that we saw in these Southern states if you haven't built the kind of infrastructure that can tap into a moment like this, and turn voters out, and make sure that you're helping to remind them, you have the power. Here's how to use the power. And you go to every one of their doors. You call everyone. You text everyone. You send them a digital ad. BURNETT: Well, you were pointing out in Florida Republicans, and I've seen these numbers over months, over these past couple years, you had registered more Republicans, in fact, like a 12-point advantage. And then you barely win either of those by, what, a point or two.

Trump won the entire state of Texas by 14 points. That's basic math. That is a swing of 26 points from Trump and a red Texas to Talarico and a blue Texas.
You had, obviously, access to your own internal numbers. I'm just curious, are you surprised by the scale of your victory?
TALARICO: I am. We saw incredible momentum on the ground over the course of the last year all over Texas. We saw a coalition coming together, first-time voters, young voters, three-time Trump voters. And so I knew that we were building something special. I didn't know we were going to win Texas by double digits. But I really do believe that the reason we won is that we forged a new kind of politics. Not a politics of hate or fear or division, but a politics of love, one that brings people together rather than pulling them apart, and I think that can be a model for our politics as a nation going forward.
BURNETT: You spoke a lot about love in, in your speech last night, and do you believe that that resonates? But hearing love as that word so front and center from a politician is extremely unusual. TALARICO: Yes. And it's the most important word in all of our lives. It's the most important word in the English language, and we rarely hear it spoken in our politics.

And I was raised to love all of my neighbors, not just my neighbors who look like me or pray like me or vote like me, and that kind of love has been missing from our politics, from our communities, from our country for too long now. And I think this election was really a response to the kind of division that we've seen over the past decade.
BURNETT: So, you're going to be the senator for a guy named Ken Paxton. Has he called you yet?

I want to serve them too. I want to fight for them too. I want to make their lives easier and better and less stressful. I am not going to Washington to represent the Democratic Party. I'm going to Washington to represent this beautiful state and every person who lives here. And so I look forward to working on a bipartisan basis to serve all Texans.
BURNETT: All right. So, that's really interesting because it was something I remember from when you and I spoke at the town hall. You said the same thing, I'm not going as a Democrat. TALARICO: I will stand up to anyone I have to to defend the people of Texas, and I will work with anyone I can to help the people of Texas. And this is what I've done for four terms in the state house. Long before this Senate race, long before I was doing interviews on CNN, I was working quietly in the halls of the legislature with both parties, with far-right Republicans and far-left Democrats, to pass 60 bipartisan bills, bills that raise teacher pay, bills that lower the cost of housing, and childcare, and property taxes, and prescription drugs, like insulin, and that's exactly what I'm going to do in the U.S. Senate.
I'm going to build bridges with all of my 99 colleagues in the U.S. Senate. I'm going to build bridges, or at least try to, with President Trump. Because my mandate is to deliver for working people in my state, to make their lives easier and better, to lower their costs, to root out corruption, and get this economy back on track.
BURNETT: You may be on an island that is not highly populated as a Democrat saying you want to work with President Trump. So, let me just ask you about that. He did say in response to this, right, I mean, the results of this election are overwhelming, Senator-Elect. He's not talking about it being rigged. He said that he would work with Democrats on things like immigration. So, are you just openly saying, yes, you will work with President Trump on something like immigration?
TALARICO: Absolutely. We have masked men in unmarked vehicles kidnapping people off our street here in the state of Texas. We have federal agents murdering our neighbors in cold blood, like Lorenzo Salgado in Houston, Texas. And so we have to take action to protect our neighbors across this state, and I will work with anyone and everyone to make that possible.
I think there's an opportunity here to finally pass comprehensive immigration reform to secure our border, I'm a border security Democrat, and also create more legal pathways for hardworking immigrants to come to this country, contribute to this economy, and make us richer and stronger as a nation, and pass a pathway to citizenship for our neighbors who have been here for years, for decades, so they can come out of the shadows.
I have not given up hope that that kind of comprehensive immigration reform is possible, and I will work with anyone to get it done.
BURNETT: You may be walking into a Washington where defying any expectations that anyone had Democrats have a majority in the House of possibly 250 seats. Right now, we're calling 231. In that world impeaching the president of the United States could be a very real thing. It could happen in the House. It could come to the Senate. Are you open to that?
TALARICO: Well, obviously, as a senator, I wouldn't be charging anyone with impeachment, but I would be sitting in an impeachment trial, which I've done before. I sat in an impeachment trial as a state representative, and I reviewed every shred of evidence. I listened to every expert. And I did my duty accordingly, and that's exactly what I would do in the U.S. Senate.
I will say, though, I think our top priority has to be delivering for the people of Texas, for the people of this country. They didn't send us to Washington for political retribution. They sent us to Washington to lower costs, to root out corruption, to get this economy back on track, and that needs to be our focus.
I really do believe that the people of this country are giving Democrats one last chance to deliver for them, to make their lives easier and better. And that's what we have to accomplish.
BURNETT: One big person, of course, in all of this here in Texas and for the entire country is Elon Musk. TALARICO: Like I said, I'll work with anyone. I think last night, we showed that all the money in the world --

BURNETT: Yes, yes, absolutely. And you talk about new Southern Democrats. I mean, you could have a Democrat from Mississippi, and if you do, it's going to make a razor-thin look like a wide margin.

SHORT: I don't think that's surprising. I think Mike Johnson did a great job of holding the conference together with incredibly slim margins over the last couple years. I think when Mike Johnson made comments that he would run for Minority Leader, it was probably intended to kind of keep the jockeying internally between Jim Jordan and Steve Scalise at bay while we were still trying to hold onto the House in November. So I've always assumed that if we actually didn't, that he probably would move on, especially with the margin this significant. It may actually be hard to gather the votes right now.
BURNETT: So now we just got a couple developments here. Two people are going to be running, at least, at least, for that Minority Leader role. Congressman Steve Scalise just announced that, and Jim Jordan just announced that.

SEN. KIRSTEN GILLIBRAND (D-NY): It's incredible. And in answer to your colleague's question, it's a blue tsunami. It's a tsunami.
That's what's bigger than a wave.
BLITZER: That's the "New York Post" front page.
GILLIBRAND: "New York Post" front page in New York City. New Yorkers are waking up to this. It was an incredible night.
People voted for change. They are fed up with Trump's America. They don't like the high cost of gasoline and heating fuel.
They don't like the high cost of groceries and housing. And they want access to better health care. They want things to be better than it is.
President Trump promised no forever wars. We're mired in this war in Iran. President Trump has delivered so much corruption to Washington.
He's much more focused on his ballrooms and tax breaks for billionaires and bombs for his war than actually getting people the health care they need to survive. And so people voted for change, and it was a blue tsunami.
BLITZER: So what does a Democratic majority in the Senate allow you to do now? What do you expect will happen?
GILLIBRAND: We can deliver for the American people.
BLITZER: How?
GILLIBRAND: Well, we can try very hard to end this war in Iran so we can get gas prices down and get home heating costs down.
We can work very hard to end these chaotic tariffs so we can get food costs and housing costs back down. We can actually fight to get better health care for Americans. All those cuts to Medicaid, all those cuts to Medicare that Trump made, we need to make sure people have access to good, affordable health care so they can survive and thrive.
And so we need to deliver for the American people by ending this war, ending these tariffs, getting costs down, and dealing with the corruption that Donald Trump has put into Washington.
BLITZER: With the impressive Democratic majority now in the Senate, you can pass this kind of legislation, but Trump could veto it, and then you'd need a two-thirds vote in the Senate to override that veto.
GILLIBRAND: Well, some of these things are so common sense, Wolf. I wouldn't be surprised if they are bipartisan. We can get people access to the health care they need, better health care, vision and dental, hearing, things that our seniors don't have, making sure people can access the medicines that their doctors prescribe them.
These are common sense, arguably bipartisan ideas that we can get done. But really taking on this war in Iran, it's an unnecessary, unauthorized, unwanted war. The American people do not support it, and it is constantly driving up our gasoline costs.
Our diesel costs are above $6 a gallon. That drives up food costs. People are tired of not being able to feed their families.
And you add to that all the corruption of Trump and cutting food stamps and cutting programs that people need, like Meals on Wheels and early childhood education. People are fed up. That's why they voted for change.
It's why they elected a whole new slate of incredible Senate candidates across the country in red and purple places that you would never expect. I mean, we are on the verge of winning Mississippi right now. We won South Carolina.
We won Texas, Iowa, Ohio. These states are red, red, red states, and it shows that the American people voted for change.
BLITZER: As you know, I grew up in New York State, in beautiful Buffalo, New York, my hometown. What, if anything, can you now do to end this tariff war with Canada? Considering the fact that New York State borders Canada.
GILLIBRAND: Yes, when you have a tariff war with Canada, it means all the inputs that you would get from Canada, whether it's building materials, whether it's pharmaceuticals, whether it's any type of food that we trade back and forth, it's more expensive. And so New Yorkers have been struggling and suffering, which is why you saw some change elections in New York as well. We just won New York 17, which is exciting.
And New York 21 is very close. They're still counting. So people want change, Wolf.
It's really that simple. And they want a government that works for them to make life more affordable so they can provide for their families. They've been suffering under Trump's America, and they rejected him wholeheartedly last night.
BLITZER: Will you support your fellow New Yorker, Chuck Schumer, to be the next majority leader in the Senate?
GILLIBRAND: I certainly will, and he's done such an incredible job. He has not only helped marshal these incredible candidates, but he's raised over half a billion dollars for these candidates. He's traveled all across the country to help create this majority, and he's done a great job.
So, yes, I am supporting Chuck fully.
BLITZER: And what was your reaction to Governor Hochul's win in New York?
GILLIBRAND: It was a big win. She crushed it. Hochul has taken on Trump from the beginning of his administration.
She's fought back on policies out of Washington that harm New York. She's fighting every day to get housing costs and grocery costs and childcare costs down in our state.
[10:45:00]
She understands what New Yorkers are going through, and she's been a really tough fighter for us, which is why she did so well last night.
BLITZER: Senator, Senator, thanks so much for joining us.
GILLIBRAND: Thanks, Wolf.
BLITZER: And congratulations to you and all your fellow Democrats.
GILLIBRAND: Thank you.
BLITZER: Appreciate it. Minutes from now, we're expecting to hear directly from the Senate minority leader, Chuck Schumer. We'll see what he has to say about the Democrats' huge win last night.
Stay with us. (COMMERCIAL BREAK)
BLITZER: We're back with our breaking election coverage. We're watching it very closely. Right now I want to bring in our excellent panelists, S.E. Cupp.
[10:50:00]
You heard James Talarico just tell our Erin Burnett, that he wants to work with President Trump to get things done. The president told Politico last night that he wants to work with Democrats after earlier insisting, he'd become Mr. Veto if the Democrats won control of Congress. What are you expecting?
S.E. CUPP, CNN POLITICAL COMMENTATOR: It's going to be rough. It's going to be a rough two years. The president's going in as Mr. Veto. We heard the incoming House Oversight Chair, Robert Garcia, saying lawyer up. So I think there's going to be some sense of defensive crouches and digging in. But James Talarico's right.
What he said to Erin was people are not sending us, meaning these newbies, to Washington for retribution. They're sending us to solve their problems. And I think that's what people expect them to do.
Now, does that mean they should ignore the corruption? No. I think there's a difference, and I think voters make this distinction too, between addressing the corruption and then the performance of impeachment.
And I think a lot of voters see the impeachment efforts as a performance that doesn't really solve a problem. They want the corruption held accountable. They want that tackled.
But they also want, finally, for lawmakers to be addressing their very real problems. It feels like lawmakers for the past decade have been going to Congress to get famous, to grift, to make money, to win favor with the president. It's time for people to do their job and remember that they are first and foremost public servants.
BLITZER: Good point indeed. You know, David, it's interesting. The breaking news we're following, the outgoing, now outgoing, House Speaker Mike Johnson telling his staff a little while ago he's not going to run for Senate minority leader because the Republicans are going to be in the minority, the GOP leader in the Senate.
But it does look like Steve Scalise and Jim Jordan will be candidates for that job. Who do you think should lead the House caucus?
CUPP: Someone from Ohio for sure, right?
DAVID URBAN, REPUBLICAN STRATEGIST AND FORMER TRUMP CAMPAIGN ADVISER: Far be it for me to tell the House members to do. But listen, both men are very talented. They both bring different skill sets.
Jim Jordan is very combative. I think probably, you know, if you're asking me who I think is going to win, I think Jim Jordan is probably going to end up being the minority leader.
BLITZER: Why do you think he'll win?
URBAN: Maybe because, you know, he led that oversight. He's a fighter. The caucus likes him, I think, more in that role.
Steve Scalise is a little bit better in terms of, you know, he's more of a consensus builder. Yes, he's a lighter touch. He's not a boxer.
He's not a wrestler. He's not a grappler. He's not there to do that.
You know, to S.E.'s point about does the president want to veto? Is he going to work with things? I think it will be an interesting mix.
You know, I think there's a world in which he could work with Chuck Schumer and Hakeem Jeffries to get things done. But, you know, to her point about oversight, if they are continually up there investigating his family and the perceived grift, he's going to be less inclined to cut deals if he feels he's under siege.
BLITZER: That's interesting. You know, Jamie, President Trump spent the last several weeks out there on the campaign trail, stumping for Republican candidates. Overnight, he said this, and I'm quoting him now.
"I wasn't on the ballot, and if I was, we would have won in a landslide." After last night, what does the president's role look like moving ahead in the coming year?
JAMIE GANGEL, CNN SPECIAL CORRESPONDENT: So first of all, let's just fact check the president, and I want to quote my friend and one of our colleagues, Peter Baker of "The New York Times," who actually counted the number of times that President Trump pleaded with voters to vote, to pretend he's on the ballot, and the answer is 47 times at least. Peter counted, so we don't have to. Just to go back for a minute to who's going to be the next minority leader, I would add one thing to what David said.
Donald Trump wants Jim Jordan to be the next minority leader. He wants someone who's going to be combative.
BLITZER: But don't you think that Trump's credibility has really gone down as a result of what happened yesterday?
AUDIE CORNISH, CNN ANCHOR: I mean, it's hard to say credibility. People believed him, right?
BLITZER: But a lot of Republicans lost because of him.
CORNISH: They did, but everyone believed him. Everybody experienced the policies, promises kept. All of those things are true.
The problem is nobody enjoyed them in their execution, and now they are reaping the fruits of that, and I think that's really difficult. To your point about Hakeem Jeffries and what to work on in the future, keep in mind there are all kinds of issues that have surfaced. Data centers is one.
Where there is a bipartisan sense of, oh boy, should we do something? And Trump, who had been palling around with big tech, telling people they were backwards for not wanting data centers, again, he was tone deaf to an issue that had grown in a bipartisan way in terms of its backlash.
Right now I'm looking at ballot measures. Something like 40 of them are moratoriums or restrictions on data centers, and most of them pass.
[10:55:00]
So I do think housing, data centers, we heard them also talking about health care. Those are things Democrats can act on and possibly draw Republicans with them. Where it gets harder, stuff like gas prices, right? Like things that we know fundamentally policy-wise, that's a lot harder.
BLITZER: All right, everybody stand by. We've got a lot more to discuss. The Senate seat in Michigan remains in Democratic hands. We're going to break down Abdul El-Sayed's massive victory in what was a bruising and chaotic election cycle.
Stay with us. There's lots going on.
(COMMERCIAL BREAK)
BLITZER: I'm Wolf Blitzer and this is CNN's special live coverage of ELECTION DAY IN AMERICA CONTINUED. Democrats have a truly historic night as they take back the House and the Senate.
BURNETT: And good morning, I'm Erin Burnett in Austin, Texas.
